#5049. Could Ultra-Processed Foods Rewire Children’s Brains?

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 4, 2026 | Reviewed by Kaja Perina

Could foods children eat change their brains? Can repeated exposure to sugar and ultra-processed foods alter children’s developing reward circuits in ways resembling the effects of addictive drugs?

An October 2026 study in JAMA Network Open provides an opportunity to revisit pioneering experiments by Bart Hoebel, Nicole Avena, and colleagues.

First, the new study. Kathleen A. Page and other investigators at Keck USC studied 46 healthy-weight children, averaging 8.7 years old. She measured ultra-processed food consumption, examined brain responses to food cues using functional MRI, and followed changes in weight and body fat over one year. According to the CDC, ultra-processed foods (UPFs) are low in dietary fiber, energy-dense, and contain little or no whole foods. They also include high amounts of sweeteners, salt, and unhealthy fats.

Children consuming more ultra-processed foods showed greater activation of the dorsal striatum, a brain region involved in reinforcement learning, motivation, and habitual behavior. Greater activation was associated with subsequent increases in body mass index and adiposity. Proof of food addiction? Not yet.

Increased activation does not always mean pathological reward processing. But could repeated exposure to highly rewarding foods alter how developing brains learn what to seek and consume?

More than three decades ago, Bart Hoebel, Nicole Avena, and colleagues at Princeton investigated whether sugar could produce addiction-like responses in laboratory rats. The rats really liked sugar. No surprise. So do most people.

But repeated cycles of sugar access, bingeing, and deprivation produced something more interesting: escalating consumption, withdrawal-like responses, and neurochemical adaptations.

Sugar bingeing repeatedly stimulated dopamine release in the nucleus accumbens. Endogenous opioid systems were also involved. In one remarkable experiment, naloxone (Narcan) precipitated withdrawal-like signs in sugar-binging rats. Sugar isn’t an opioid. Yet opioid-related mechanisms help make sugar so appealing.

The Princeton experiments established that particular patterns of sugar exposure could produce addiction-like adaptations in animals. The USC study asked if children's habitual UPF diets induce differences in developing reward circuits.

I subsequently collaborated with Hoebel and Avena, whom I mentored at the University of Florida, to investigate the overlap between drug addiction and excessive eating.

In 2011, Avena and I published “Food and Addiction—Sugars, Fats and Hedonic Overeating.” With Hoebel and Miriam Bocarsly, we also examined overlapping diagnostic concepts involving substance abuse and overeating.

Our question was simple: Why do people continue eating when no longer hungry? Maybe people really like how the food makes them feel- “hedonic eating.”

Nicole Avena, PhD, Professor of Neuroscience at the Icahn School of Medicine at Mount Sinai and author of Sugarless, continues investigating these relationships.

Dr. Avena recently talked to me about the scientific challenge in studying overeating. Said Avena, “Food engages the same reward circuitry that drugs do--that part isn't controversial anymore. What we're still working out is which foods, how much, and at what age repeated exposure starts to change how the brain responds."

That distinction matters. It seems most important when discussing children and their development of eating habits and preferences.

Avena adds, "Calling a food addictive doesn't mean it's the same as cocaine. It means some of the same brain mechanisms (reward, craving, loss of control) can show up with food, and that's worth taking seriously."

But the brain does not maintain an entirely separate reward system for every rewarding substance.

Paul Kenny's lab extended this research, demonstrating reward dysfunction, altered dopamine signaling, and compulsive-like eating in animals exposed to highly palatable diets.

Dopamine participates in motivation, learning, and reinforcement. Endogenous opioids contribute to reward and hedonic responses. Learned cues may provoke seeking long after physiological needs have been satisfied.

My interest began in the 1970s studying cigarette smoking-induced weight loss and then cessation-related gain. Then, in the early ‘80s, I studied patients using cocaine or amphetamines who said they lost weight because they simply forgot to eat. But during abstinence, appetite returned.

Drugs changed eating and appetite. Stopping drugs changes that back again with a vengeance.

With University of Florida colleagues, I summarized these relationships in 2011. Subsequent experimental research with Orsini, Avena, and colleagues examined increased food consumption and weight gain following chronic amphetamine exposure.

Clinical observations suggested overlapping mechanisms involving dopamine, withdrawal, reinforcement, and appetite regulation.

In 2007, Kelly Brownell and I co-chaired the Yale Conference on Food and Addiction, bringing together nearly 40 experts. Nora Volkow delivered the keynote address. Brownell and I subsequently edited Food and Addiction: A Comprehensive Handbook. Ashley Gearhardt and colleagues developed the Yale Food Addiction Scale, permitting researchers to measure addiction-like eating behaviors.

At the time, comparing food and addiction was controversial. The accepted view was that food was necessary for survival. Cocaine was not.

True. But that was never the scientific question.

Kevin Hall's randomized inpatient study demonstrated that adults consumed approximately 500 additional calories daily on an ultra-processed diet compared with a minimally processed diet.

Importantly, obesity is not synonymous with food addiction. Some obese people don’t demonstrate addictive eating. Others show addiction-like eating without obesity.

Epigenetics offers another possible connection with eating patterns. Repeated exposure to cocaine, opioids, and other addictive drugs can produce persistent epigenetic changes in gene expression within reward circuits.

Eric Nestler and colleagues showed that addictive drugs alter gene regulation in reward circuits without changing DNA sequences. Animal studies suggest highly palatable diets may produce similar adaptations. These epigenetic changes are important, suggesting that highly rewarding diets like drugs similarly influence gene regulation and neural plasticity.

But these changes are not necessarily permanent.

Could repeated exposure to particular foods create a biological memory that makes them more difficult to resist? Possibly

Ultra-processed foods account for two-thirds of the calories American children consume, according to CDC experts. These often combine refined carbohydrates, fats, flavors, and textures engineered for palatability.

The child’s developing brain learns through repeated experience, shaping learning, preferences, and habits.

Not all ultra-processed foods are equivalent. The important questions concern which foods, which children, which developmental periods, and which exposures produce persistent effects.

The October study is provocative but small. Dietary intake was estimated, neuroimaging occurred once, and confounding remains possible.

Though we are accumulating impressive MRI data, association is not causation.

The science has advanced beyond our original experiments. The fundamental question remains: When does hedonic or rewarding eating become compulsive eating?

We need studies examining genetic vulnerability, early stress, food insecurity, and specific combinations of sugar and fat.

The objective is not to diagnose eight-year-olds with food addiction.

The question is no longer if food engages the brain's reward circuitry. Of course it does.

The question is whether repeated exposure to particular foods during childhood produces lasting changes in motivation, habits, and vulnerability to compulsive eating.

And whether those changes can be prevented—or reversed.

Children cannot choose the food environment we have created for them. Adults can.

#5047. Tesla turns to car rental platform Turo for Full Self-Driving test drives

Turo will soon let US renters search specifically for Teslas with “Full Self-Driving” (Supervised) turned on, using a direct integration with Tesla to verify each car’s FSD status. The feature goes live on October 28.

For Tesla, it’s a new way to put FSD in front of people who don’t own a Tesla, and a new source of the FSD subscriptions that now count toward Elon Musk’s pay package.

Hosts link their Tesla account to Turo, and the platform pulls the car’s FSD status through Tesla’s API. Eligible listings then show that FSD is active, and guests can filter for it.

Turo says “thousands” of FSD-enabled Teslas are already listed. Not a shocker. The Model 3 and Model Y are the third and fourth most popular vehicles on Turo so far this year, according to the company.

Eligible US hosts can also get a free one-month FSD (Supervised) trial. After that, it’s Tesla’s regular $99 per month subscription, a cost hosts will want to recover through their daily rates. Turo didn’t say whether FSD listings will carry a premium.

Turo CEO Andre Haddad called it an “industry-first integration with Tesla” and said that “most travelers have had limited opportunity to experience driver’s assistance technology, but the demand is clear.”

The “industry-first” part is generous. Tesla has been renting out its own cars with FSD included in Southern California since last year, a program we first spotted in the works back in 2023. The new part is verified FSD on a third-party marketplace with far more cars in far more cities.

Tesla went subscription-only with FSD in February, and since then it has been pushing hard to get people to try it, including a free one-year subscription on new Model 3 and Model Y orders.

There’s a reason for the urgency. Tesla reported 1.74 million “active FSD subscriptions” in Q3, a metric it only started disclosing because Musk’s 2025 CEO Performance Award uses it. One of the award’s milestones is 10 million. At last quarter’s pace, Tesla would need about eight more years to get there.

Turo’s release leans heavily on “self-driving” and “driverless” language. It cites its own research claiming that 55% of Americans are interested in “driverless technology” for travel, rising to 71% among Gen Z, and that over a fifth of interested Americans would be comfortable spending seven or more hours in a self-driving car.

But FSD (Supervised) is not driverless. It’s a level 2 driver assistance system, and the person behind the wheel is responsible at all times. Even Haddad calls it “driver’s assistance technology” in the same release.

Renters will be getting into a car they’ve likely never driven, with a system they’ve likely never used. Turo’s safeguard is to encourage guests to review Tesla’s FSD documentation and safety guidelines before pickup. That’s it.

We saw what over-trust looks like just two weeks ago, when a driver told the CHP he was asleep with FSD engaged before his Tesla hit a patrol car inside a lane closure in Fairfield, California. Two officers were seriously injured.

Turo also didn’t say who’s on the hook when a guest crashes with FSD engaged: the renter, the host, or Turo’s protection plans.

This is basically ‘Tesla network’ but supervised. Since 2016, Elon Musk has been saying that Tesla owners are going to be able to put their vehicles in the ‘Tesla Network’ to provide rides for others and earn income.

This vision has been delayed for years, but the best they can do right now is combining Turo and FSD.

Usually when I rent a Tesla on Turo the owner has turned off all self steering features. So far, they appear to be more confident in my driving than letting FSD do the work. Given the risks of letting a person unfamiliar with your car drive it, I think if they sincerely believed FSD was safer they would have it on all the time and not let me near the steering wheel. This speaks volumes about FSD.

For Tesla, it should be good for FSD adoption. A weekend road trip with FSD is a much better test drive than 15 minutes around the block with a Tesla advisor, and Turo gets a feature no rental company has.

However, I don’t want to be a party pooper, but I do worry about the complacency issue. Turo is pitching “self-driving” to travelers, and Tesla still calls the product “Full Self-Driving” in the US, even after it dropped the name in Europe under pressure from German regulators. Put a tourist who has never used FSD behind the wheel with that framing, and some of them will treat it as a chauffeur. That’s exactly how you end up with a driver asleep at freeway speed.

At a minimum, Turo should require a short in-app FSD walkthrough before a guest can use it, and spell out liability before someone finds out the hard way. Tesla, which collects the subscriptions, should want the same.

If a weekend in a rented Tesla convinces you to go electric, powering your own EV with home solar can cut your fuel costs to nearly zero. With electricity rates up almost 10% last year and expected to keep climbing, going solar is one of the best ways to protect yourself against rising costs. #5045. Cybertruck driver stopped in Germany, ‘Monster truck’ not allowed on roads

A Tesla Cybertruck was pulled over while driving in Germany, a reminder that the vehicle is generally not legal on European roads.

The Tesla Cybertruck has attracted plenty of attention, mostly for its unconventional and polarizing look. For better or worse*, the car looks different than anything else on the road, and thus has been the target of all sorts of discussion (*it’s for worse).

But it’s not just the look of the car that’s unconventional. Because of that look, it also breaks several European rules, particularly those related to pedestrian protection (which was called out immediately upon reveal by many observers, including this author).

As a result, it does not have “type approval” in Germany. Even applications for individual exemptions have been denied because of the vehicle “deviates significantly” from requirements.

The vehicle is also too large. In general, a “class B” license, the normal license you get for driving a car, allows you to drive a vehicle up to 3,500kg (7,716lb). The Cybertruck does weigh less than this, but this number refers to the “Gross Vehicle Weight,” or the maximum weight of the truck when laden with occupants and cargo, for which all trims of the Cybertruck exceed 3,500kg.

A Swedish driver ran up against this barrier when driving through Bavaria, near the town of Vaterstatten, Merkur.de reports (with the funny title “Tesla monster truck stopped on A99”). The driver was holding a class B license, like most drivers, whereas the police said that a C1 license allowing operation of a vehicle up to 7.5 tons would be required.

The police did not allow the driver to continue on their journey. Vaterstatten is near the Southern border of Germany, so the Cybertruck might not have been on German roads for long… except the driver is Swedish, so if they drove all the way from Sweden, they would have spent quite a while on German roads before being stopped.

German here: Technically it is possible to register a modified Cybertruck in a European country and drive it legally on European roads. But you can't register it in Germany, you are only allowed to drive it here occasionally and for less than one year. And you still need a truck driver license (typ "C1" for up to 7.5 tonnes) or an older German license "Führerscheinklasse 3", and it has to be limited to max 90 km/h (approx 56 mph).

This guy from Sweden has somehow managed to register it in Poland. Still the car was not limited in top speed and he didn't have the necessary license for more than 3,5 t maximum weight. So he wasn't allowed to drive any further and probably had to pay high fines.

We’ve seen similar situations before in Europe, with an imported Cybertruck being seized in the UK, someone trying to smuggle two through Lithuania into Russia, and with the US Army warning servicemembers that the Cybertruck is not allowed in Germany.

Nevertheless a few people still manage to get Cybertrucks onto European roads, by getting individual approval from one country or another, then driving across borders. But even in cases like that, a story like this shows that drivers could open themselves up to trouble by driving this unapproved vehicle on European roads.

#5043. What Is The Bare Minimum Amount Of Strength Training You Need To Do For It To Be Effective? Here's What Trainers Say

Right up there with a balanced diet, strength training is the OG biohack for longevity, and the key to a strong, healthy body. But whether you’re a beginner or seasoned athlete, that begs the question: How much strength training do you actually need to see results?

If you look to the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention for answers, they say that adults need at least two days of “muscle-strengthening activity" per week for their overall health. But, resistance training even once a week was enough to make progress for beginners, per a study in BMC Sports Science, Medicine and Rehabilitation.

"While it may seem the small amount of volume would be insignificant, this study demonstrates that some exercise is better than no exercise,” says certified personal trainer Natalya Vasquez. But as your skill level progresses, you’ll need to increase the intensity and volume of your workouts to elicit more muscle growth.

Below, experts explain exactly how much strength training is enough based on your fitness level and goals.

Meet the experts: Tina Tang, CPT, is a certified personal trainer, strength coach, and founder of Iron Strong Fitness. Natalya Vasquez, CPT, is a certified personal trainer, health coach, and founder of On Your Terms Coaching.

The following cheat-sheet will give you an idea of how many days a week you should strength train.

While once a week was enough for the folks in the aforementioned study to make progress, they only looked at a single exercise: the leg press. Vasquez says that ideally, beginners looking to build total-body muscle and strength should start with two, or even three, nonconsecutive strength training sessions per week.

This frequency helps build a habit (key for those just starting out) and provides your body with the necessary stimulus for initial muscle growth, while also allowing for plenty of time to recover. But, your sessions don't have to be long: Initially, each session can be as short as 15 to 20 minutes, and you can gradually increase to 45- to 60-minute workouts as you build endurance, says certified personal trainer Tina Tang, CPT.

As for how you structure your workouts, Vasquez says you can either do two to three full-body workouts, or a three-day split, like a push/pull/legs workout, where your training is organized by muscle group.

For a full-body routine, Vasquez suggests completing one exercise per muscle group for three to five sets of five to eight reps per workout. For a three-day split, complete three to four exercises for the same muscle group for two to three sets of five to eight reps.

Vasquez likes the five to eight rep range because she says this helps develop functional hypertrophy: “We don’t just want to gain muscle, we want to have actual strength where this muscle growth is functional,” she says.

“You don’t need complicated gym equipment to get results,” says Vasquez. “Dumbbells and bands are affordable, convenient, and versatile options for exercising at home, and as your strength increases, gym access may be beneficial since it’ll give you additional equipment and a wider selection of weights.”

In this case, the trainers suggest strength training three times a week, and depending on the intensity and volume of the workout, each session should range between 30 to 60 minutes, Tang says. “This frequency is effective for preserving muscle mass and strength while ensuring adequate recovery and adaptation.”

However, overall volume may be more important that days per week, according to a 2021 review in Sports Medicine. The researchers conclude that it's possible to see gains by training anywhere from one to four days per week so long as you're getting in at least four sets of six to 15 reps per muscle group per week.

The key here will be to follow the principle of progressive overload. Gradually increasing the weight, sets and or reps, over time will continually challenge the muscles, stimulate growth, and improve bone density, says Tang. “The body craves movement, and once it's accustomed to a certain level of activity, it needs more of a challenge to keep making gains."

Good news: The longer you’ve been strength training, the less work you’ll need to maintain your gains since your muscles are composed of more fibers, says Vasquez. “So if you’ve been strength training five times per week for a decade and then something happens in your life that doesn’t allow you to work out as much, you’ll still be able to maintain your muscle with one full-body workout per week,” she says. However, this would be the bare minimum, and resistance training two to three times a week is still the ideal sweet spot.

In terms of the type of training, Tang also recommends full-body workouts to maintain balanced muscle development and overall strength. Look to hit all the major movement patterns for best results. For each exercise, Vasquez says to aim to complete three to five sets of five to eight reps.

Aside from logging miles, strength training at least twice a week is a crucial part of every running regimen, says Tang. “Ideally, one session can focus on bilateral, foundational movements, while a second session allows for a more specialized approach, targeting unilateral exercises, core stability, and hip strength,” she explains. “This balance ensures you're not only building overall strength but also addressing the specific needs that support running performance and injury prevention.”

Indeed, the addition of two to four strength training sessions a week helped mid- to long-distance runners prevent injury and run faster, according to a 2024 meta-analysis in the journal Sports Medicine.

Workouts should range between 30 to 60 minutes and rep ranges can vary from five to eight (with heavier weight and for more advanced strength trainers) to 12 to 20 reps (for runners newer to strength training and using lighter weights), Vasquez says.

FWIW, the meta-analysis notes that plyometric training and "high load" strength training (using weights at 80% of your one rep max or heavier), specifically, improved running economy.

For this goal, Tang says to strength train at least twice a week. Doing so "helps build and maintain muscle mass and bone density, which is crucial for protecting bones and joints as we age, and this frequency provides a balance between stimulating the muscles and allowing for adequate recovery,” she says.

Plus, regular resistance training helps strengthen tendons and ligaments, in turn, supporting joint stability and overall musculoskeletal health, she adds.

Full-body workouts and compound exercises like deadlifts, overhead presses, and squats are ideal since they involve multiple joints and mimic how your body moves in real life, says Vasquez. Depending on your experience, this could look like choosing two to three exercises and doing three to five sets for five to eight reps (to focus on building functional strength) per workout.

No matter your goals, strength training is certainly worth your time. Aside from supporting your overall wellbeing, picking up weights can prevent injury, improve metabolic health, reduce the risk of cardiovascular disease, and support bone density, especially in women, says Vasquez.

In fact, approximately one in five women over age 50 have osteoporosis (a condition that causes bones to become weak and brittle), but strength training can help enhance bone density and reduce the risk of fractures, says Tang.

Sarcopenia, an age-related progressive loss of muscle mass strength, is another common condition among women, and after age 30, you can gradually begin losing muscle mass each decade. Fortunately, strength training can again help combat the effects and reverse muscle loss, says Tang.

Finally, regular weight training supports balance, mobility, and functional strength which are crucial for performing daily activities and living independently, Tang says. “Strength training acts as an investment in our future health, ensuring that we maintain physical strength and resilience for the long term.”

She is a graduate of Emory University and Northwestern University's Medill School of Journalism. Her work has appeared in Women's Health, POPSUGAR, Food & Wine, What To Expect, Cosmopolitan, Men's Health, and elsewhere. As a former collegiate pole vaulter, she loves all things fitness and has yet to meet a group workout class she doesn't like.

#5040. Do We Get More Generous as We Age?

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 4, 2026 | Reviewed by Monica Vilhauer Ph.D.

Most of us are more willing to help people we feel close to. For example, we’ll give freely to a sibling, but think a little longer about a coworker, and hesitate more with someone we barely know. Psychologists call this social discounting — the value we place on another person’s welfare shrinks as social distance grows

A new study of adults in the United States and China suggests this economic phenomenon varies across age groups. For example, older adults are more generous to people with whom they have weaker social connections.

Yi Lu, an assistant professor of psychology at Western Kentucky University, set out to better understand if generosity varies across age. Lu earned her Ph.D. in developmental psychology at Cornell working with professor Corinna E. Löckenhoff, and collaborated on the study with colleagues at The Chinese University of Hong Kong and Peking University.

The research team asked nearly 1,000 adults of various ages from the U.S. and China to make a series of hypothetical decisions about how much money they would share with different people, from a close family member to a complete stranger. Participants also reported their charitable donations. The researchers then compared age groups while accounting for participants' cultural values and other background differences.

In both countries, older adults were the most generous. Compared with younger adults, they were less influenced by social distance (more willing to help acquaintances and strangers) and they gave more to charity.

Lu points out that many people assume older adults become more self-focused or simply have less to give as they age. But, she says, the data told a different story. Because the pattern appeared in two different cultural settings, it may reflect a broader pattern about how people change with age.

While both Chinese and American older adults were more likely to give than younger adults, there were some cultural differences. Regardless of age, people who placed greater value on equality and closeness with others tended to be more generous. Those who placed greater value on personal achievement and status tended to be less generous. Taken together, the findings suggest that generosity in adulthood reflects both how old we are and the values we hold.

Lu notes several limitations to this study. The questions were hypothetical, so they capture what people said they would do, not necessarily what they would do with real money in the moment; and the real charitable donations were self-reported. The study also compared people of different ages at one point in time. That shows older and younger adults differ in their generosity, but it doesn’t prove that people become more generous as they age, because generational differences may play a role.

The study also doesn’t explain why older adults give more, Lu notes. Researchers have some ideas, such as a shift toward emotionally meaningful goals later in life, but there isn’t concrete evidence that points to a reason.

“This is one of the few studies to look at how people of different ages share resources with others across social distances and across cultures,” Lu said. “In both the U.S. and China, older adults in our study were more willing to extend generosity beyond their close circle. That matters for how we think about aging. Older adults aren’t just recipients of support; they can be an important source of generosity in their communities.”

The take-home message: Older adults are more likely to give to people beyond their social circles than compared to younger adults. And no matter the age, people who value equality and closeness tend to be more generous overall.

#5037. Tesla makes another joke of its Robotaxi geofence expansion

Tesla expanded its Robotaxi service area in Houston today, and it shaped the new geofence like a rocket. Plenty of people on X see something else in it, which is fair given the phallic Austin map Tesla posted in 2025.

It’s a cute nod to Houston’s space history. It’s also a service area drawn for a meme, and Robotaxi Tracker counts only 34 driverless Model Ys to cover it.

Tesla’s official Robotaxi account announced the expansion on X this morning with “Houston, we have liftoff” and a map comparing the old and new geofences:

Houston, we have liftoff Bigger service area now live pic.twitter.com/ps8fZmWpuP

When Tesla launched in Houston in April, the service covered a small zone of about 25 square miles in northwest Harris County, around Jersey Village, Willowbrook and a stretch of FM 1960. It was miles away from downtown Houston.

The new geofence turns that zone into the nose cone and adds a long body running southeast to downtown and Bellaire, where it flares out into fins. Pair that with “Houston, we have liftoff,” a riff on the famous Apollo 13 line, and the theme isn’t subtle.

The problem is that a long rocket drawn diagonally across a map also looks a lot like something else, and Tesla has been here before. In July 2025, less than a month into the Austin pilot, Tesla expanded its Austin geofence into a shape that people immediately compared to a penis. Instead of shrugging it off, Elon Musk leaned in:

Bigger, longer and uncut 😂 https://t.co/VmAfkdndMI

That post got about 30 million views. It didn’t age great either. Days later, Waymo expanded its own Austin territory to 90 square miles and was running roughly 100 times more vehicles than Tesla, as we reported at the time. Tesla still had employees riding in every car back then.

Robotaxi Tracker, which crowdsources sightings of Tesla’s Robotaxi vehicles, now lists Tesla’s Houston service area at about 254 square miles, roughly 10 times the launch zone.

The fleet hasn’t grown nearly as fast. The tracker counts 34 unsupervised Model Ys in Houston, up from 22 three months ago, and no Cybercabs yet. That works out to about one car for every 7.5 square miles.

That’s the same pattern we saw in Austin earlier this year, when Tesla expanded “Robotaxi” to the entire Austin metro with only ~20 vehicles. To be fair, Austin has since caught up: the tracker now counts 397 driverless vehicles there, more than half of them Cybercabs.

Waymo has run fully driverless rides there since February and roughly doubled its Houston service area to nearly 50 square miles in May, ahead of the World Cup.

it's just to signal that Tesla and SpaceX plan to merge. Same with the roadster event - it has nothing to do with an actual product. the roadster 2 is vaporware. The event's only purpose is to signal a merger. that's what this map is

Why? There’s no logic in making the service area in this shape. Why not just make it where you think it is safe and useful to operate? All for a joke? It makes no sense to me.

During Tesla’s earnings call last week, the company again made it clear that safety is its biggest bottleneck for Robotaxi expansion. Tesla still limits itself to lower-speed roads and avoids more complex environments; even train crossings have been an issue.

Because of that, geo-fenced areas only tell part of the story. You can now order a ride within this area, but that doesn’t mean Tesla can operate efficiently there.It is limited by the number of vehicles in operation at any given time and the routes those vehicles take. Otherwise, Tesla would have bigger exposure to potential crashes.

With electricity rates up almost 10% last year and expected to keep climbing, going solar is one of the best ways to protect yourself against rising costs. #5031. The 2027 Lexus RZ starts at under $50,000 and offers over 300 miles of range

The 2027 Lexus RZ will begin arriving at dealerships early next year, with prices starting at under $50,000.

After introducing the updated RZ last year, the electric SUV has seen significantly more demand this year.

Toyota’s luxury brand sold over 10,000 RZs through September, nearly double (+91%) the 5,340 it managed during the same period last year.

For the 2027 model year, the Lexus RZ will be available with three powertrains: 350e, 450e AWD, and 550e F Sport AWD.

The base 350e uses a 79.6 kWh battery and a 221-horsepower front motor, delivering up to 301 miles of electric range.

Upgrading to the 450e AWD model costs an extra $3,500 and starts at $50,995. The AWD variant also uses the 79.6 kWh battery but adds a rear motor with Lexus’ DIRECT4 all-wheel-drive, delivering a combined 308 horsepower and 260 miles of range.

The top-spec Lexus RZ 550e AWD starts at $58,770. The AWD setup delivers a combined 402 horsepower, which Lexus said enables it to sprint from 0 to 60 mph in 4.2 seconds, the fastest of the lineup.

The F Sport variant is also the first to feature M Mode, an electronic system that replicates the feeling of an eight-speed manual transmission.

All variants have a built-in NACS port on the passenger side, so drivers can use Tesla Superchargers without a separate adaptor. Charging the battery from 10% to 80% takes about 30 minutes.

Inside, the RZ comes with NuLuxe-trimmed seats as standard, while the Luxury and F Sport grades have Ultrasuede-trimmed seats and laser-etched Ultrasuede door trim inserts.

All 2027 RZ models also include a heated steering wheel and heated and ventilated front seats as standard.

Introducing the 2014? Lexus RZ. AWD...308 horsepower and 260 miles of range... 10-80% fast charging at an average speed of 112 kw.

Toyota may not know how to make an EV, but it is nailing this time travel thing.

The 2027 RZ comes with the latest Lexus Interface multimedia system, including a 14″ navigation screen with wireless Apple CarPlay and Android Auto.

Lexus said the 2027 RZ will begin arriving at dealerships early next year. Starting at $47,495, the RZ is $7,500 more than the Tesla Model Y ($39,990), which offers up to 321 miles of range.

#5029. The Mental Edge: 6 Tools to Use to Perform Better at Anything

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 4, 2026 | Reviewed by Monica Vilhauer Ph.D.

Mental skills, also called psychological skills, are those mental abilities that allow us to do everything from getting up off the couch, getting through a rough day, managing a pressurized situation, and realizing our potential. These can be learned and honed to meet our individualized needs, and with practice, facilitate both small and large changes in our everyday and game time performance.

Want to be more motivated? Want to learn to handle stress better? Want to improve your run times? Want to start exercising? Want to do better on tests? Want more confidence? You get the idea. These skills can help.

Some foundational mental skills for performance optimization are deep breathing, relaxation skills, self-talk, a reset routine, cognitive reframing, and visualization/imagery.

Deep breathing is a foundational mental skill to incorporate into your mental skills playbook. Deep breathing is key to physical health, mental health, pain management, stress management, emotional control, and cognitive focus in high-stakes situations. Regularly practicing deep breathing is key to being able to use it during challenging moments to refocus and gain control of your physiological response.

There is a wide array of relaxation skills, but three effective ones are deep breathing, specifically diaphragmatic breathing, progressive muscle relaxation, and meditation. Relaxation techniques decrease stress and improve stress tolerance and improve health and cognitive performance.

For getting motivated, staying focused, and accomplishing goals, there is nothing quite like self-talk. That running dialogue in our heads is extremely powerful and feeds our self-confidence, attentional focus, problem solving abilities, and ability to stick with things even when they are hard. Instructional self-talk gives us the edge we need to remember how to execute complex problems or learn a new skill. If you have ever learned the breaststroke for example, you repeated the instructional mantra “pull, breathe, kick, glide” to yourself many times. Motivational self-talk, on the other, fuels our confidence, focus, and drive. “You got this!”

When we make a mistake or get thrown off our game by an external variable, sometimes we allow ourselves to be derailed. Performance suffers; we lose focus and become less effective. With a conscious reset routine at the ready to intervene, it is possible to rebound quickly and effectively. A good reset routine is short and incorporates an acknowledgment of the mistake or issue without judgment, a slow, deep breath, and a quick instructional self-talk cue to get back on track. For example, a flight student who has made a mistake during a training flight might 1) acknowledge the mistake without judgment, 2) take a deep 5-7 breath (inhale slowly for 5 counts, exhale slowly for 7 counts), and 3) refocus with the instructional self-talk cue, “Run the next checklist.” After a mistake on the field, an athlete might 1) acknowledge the mistake without judgement, 2) take a slow, deep breath, and 3) refocus with “Next play.”

Cognitive reframing involves recognizing maladaptive/self-defeating thoughts, examining them objectively, and reframing/replacing them with a realistic and practical thought. The goal here is not unrealistic positivity, but useful and task-focused language. For example, after a mistake during flight training, a student might think “I’m terrible, I’m going to fail out.” This thought is defeatist, absolute, and catastrophic. After examining the thoughts objectively, replacements might be: “I made a common mistake and need to refocus my study time on this area. I’ve worked hard to get here and otherwise I am doing well.” Cognitive reframing or restructuring has been shown to reduce self-critical rumination, increase positive affect, and decrease negative affect in athletes. Cognitive reframing can be used in the moment and is a valuable strategy to move forward after a setback.

Visualization and imagery are techniques in which we mentally simulate a task (e.g., emergency procedures or a golf swing) in order to better execute that task when needed. Mental rehearsal stimulates neurocognitive processes in much the same way as actually engaging in the activity. Visualization has been shown to enhance mental control, improve motor skills/technical skills and increase self-confidence, and can be used alone or in conjunction with other training strategies. Visualization, imagery, and mental rehearsal are key components of elite performers, including the Navy’s Blue Angels, who routinely engage in chair flying, in which maneuvers are mentally practiced in conjunction with motor movements (see The Blue Angels documentary, 2024, produced by Amazon MGM Studios).

Benefits of mental skills are that they are free, accessible to everyone, and have high levels of effectiveness, which can be applied to virtually any aspect of life. Whether your goal is to manage everyday stress, build confidence, bounce back from setbacks, earn your wings, win a championship, or perform at your best when it matters most, these skills can make a meaningful difference. Like physical skills, mental skills require practice to become effective and readily available when you need them. Start small, choose a skill or two that fits your needs, and practice consistently. Over time, you’ll build a mental skills playbook that you can make work for you.

#5027. It Just Got Easier to Get Peptides in the US. Here's What Could Come Next.

Spoiler: More access doesn’t necessarily mean more safety.

ON WEDNESDAY, THE U.S. Food and Drug Administration announced a new policy that makes six experimental peptides more readily available. According to a document released by the HHS, the government will not penalize specialized pharmacies for making and supplying these popular substances to patients with a prescription. The peptides: BPC-157, epitalon, KPV, MOTS-c, Semax, and TB-500.

This does not mean these particular peptides have been approved for use by the FDA. In fact, none of them have gone through the rigorous human testing required for FDA drug approval. And the new policy is not intended to be permanent. Instead, it allows temporary access as the FDA decides on a longer-term plan for the six peptides. It also includes the caveat that compounders report “serious adverse events, and product quality issues to FDA and to the States where the products are compounded,” according to the document.

This topic has been red-hot since July, when the FDA held an advisory committee meeting to consider whether to add these peptides to a list of substances that U.S. compounding pharmacies are allowed to manufacture and supply. These substances are already widely used and obtained on the gray market. If people are going to use them, advocates have argued, it would be safest if those substances came from licensed pharmacies and are used with physician oversight.

Critics, on the other hand—including FDA scientists—are concerned that the government may be opening the floodgates to under-studied and potentially harmful drugs. These substances, they note, have not been fully vetted for safety and effectiveness. And some of these peptides are already being promoted and sold on the basis of scientific claims that experts say are unsupported or questionable.

Here's what could come next, if you're thinking about taking one of these six compounded peptides.

Some parts of the current conversation played out at last month’s PepMed conference in Washington, D.C. A sign pointing guests to the registration table featured a stylized gold peptide overlaid on the white dome of the U.S. Capitol. PepMed’s organizer, the American Academy of Peptide Medicine (AAPM), founded just last year, has already become a major voice in this debate.

In advance of the FDA’s July meeting, AAPM submitted hundreds of pages to a crowded public docket, including information about each of the seven peptides then under review. Several of its advisory board members spoke during the meeting’s public comment sessions. And the group appears to have the ear of the administration. PepMed speakers included the assistant secretary for health, Admiral Brian Christine, and a host of prominent MAHA figures.

The September event opened with an on-the-record media hour, featuring a dozen speakers, including physicians and owners and CEOs of compounding pharmacies and telehealth companies. A recurring theme was the danger of the gray market, where peptide quality can be difficult or impossible to verify and consumers often make treatment decisions without medical oversight—“this is not a victory for patient safety,” said Ricardo Rossello, PhD, a U.S. congressional delegate for Puerto Rico and AAPM advisory board member who co-founded Beijing Prosperous Biopharm, a medical research company.

Many of the speakers also lamented the failure of the U.S. health care system to fully meet the needs of patients, and some of the day’s speakers, including Alex Tatem, MD, an Indiana-based urologist and men’s health specialist with a large YouTube following, presented experimental peptides as part of the solution. “Our established medical system is very much a sick care model,” said Tatem, who serves on the AAPM’s medical advisory board. It’s important to help patients in a health crisis, he told me in an interview. But people want to be proactive about disease prevention. In his view, the peptides under FDA’s consideration can play a role by expanding “the toolkit that’s available to providers.”

At the FDA’s July advisory committee meeting, the agency’s scientists advised against allowing broad access to these peptides, in part because they have not been fully vetted for safety and effectiveness in the kind of long-term, rigorously designed human studies typically required for drug approval.Even at the conference, some doctors acknowledged the evidence gaps. Abud Bakri, MD, works in California as a physician who specializes in caring for hospitalized patients. He also runs a separate medical practice focused on preventive and longevity medicine, and he serves on the Enhanced Games Independent Medical Commission. His interest in peptides has been long-standing enough to predate his medical career.

Bakri, now 33, has been a powerlifter since he was 17. He explained that he has a genetic condition that may increase his risk for ligament injuries. He first used BPC-157 almost a decade ago, and believes the peptide helped him recover faster, though he acknowledges that this is hard to prove definitively, given the absence of well-designed human studies.

If just a fraction of the rat and Petri dish research holds up in humans, BPC-157 could be an incredibly helpful drug in the ICU, he told me at the conference, during an interview at an outdoor dining area. But ideally, he says, its effectiveness should be proven before it’s used in this setting and potential side effects should be better understood. “If I’m going to have an honest conversation with a patient,” he said, “I need to be able to fully elucidate the risk, which I can’t for some of these compounds.”

After the conference, I spoke by Zoom with Thomas Kremen, MD, a physician at UCLA Health who works for several athletic teams, including the Los Angeles Chargers and Lakers. Kremen and his colleagues recently reviewed the evidence behind six popular experimental peptides. He and his co-authors seemed unimpressed. “It’s probably not a strong stance to be recommending these things,” he told me.

Would it be accurate for physicians to tell their patients that a peptide like BPC-157 heals tissue?

“Maybe if they're talking about a rat in some study,” he said. “But I think if you're making a claim, you better be able to cite the literature that supports that.”

Right now, not only is there a dearth of high-quality clinical evidence that peptides work and are safe, unsupported claims are being made about these compounds.

For instance, among the documents that AAPM submitted to the FDA’s public docket was a white paper claiming that two experimental peptides—epitalon and BPC-157—are endogenous, indicating they are naturally produced by the body. Similar descriptions can be found on websites for telehealth companies and online peptide vendors suggesting their products are synthetic versions of naturally derived peptides.

Epitalon is a lab-made peptide based on an extract that researchers in the Soviet Union obtained from cattle brains. Terry Roberts, PhD, a researcher who studies epitalon at Brunel University of London, said it’s unclear whether humans produce this peptide. As far as he’s aware, no studies have explicitly described its presence in humans.

And BPC-157? The peptide was discovered in gastric juice, said Vince Luca, PhD, a researcher at the Moffitt Cancer Center in Florida. Yet 35 years after its discovery, nobody has found a gene that encodes for it—it could have come from a microbe or from food. It’s not impossible that the body produces BPC-157, said Luca, but he thinks it’s unlikely. He also says that health care professionals “should know enough not to tell [people] that it's an endogenous peptide” when it is very unlikely to be one. And regardless, he continued, “there are plenty of endogenous peptides that, injected at high concentrations, would have a really negative effect on the body.” In other words, being endogenous alone doesn’t mean it’s safe.

(The American Academy of Peptide Medicine’s press team did not respond to queries asking how the group determined that BPC-157 and epitalon are endogenous.)

“My prediction is that those who are already using the gray market will continue to do so,” said Ryan Beattie, director of business development at Adaptog Research & Life Sciences, a UK-based company that sells experimental peptides. “So I think in the initial term, people will use compounding pharmacies in the US. What I then predict will happen is people will then get used to it, get more aware of it—and then switch to the gray market because of the cost.”

He may be right. For this story, we spoke with two peptide users who had been living with chronic pain, and after trying physical therapy as well as other treatment approaches, turned to the Wolverine stack, a combination of BPC-157 and TB-500. Both said they felt the peptides helped them—and neither was sure they’d switch to compounded versions, which are more expensive than the gray market and aren’t covered by insurance. One of them, Charles A., asked, “What are the hoops and what are the costs going to be?”

A third person, who asked to be referred to as James—the name he uses in online communities—tried an oral version of BPC-157 four years ago and said he hasn’t been the same since then. Shortly after taking the experimental compound, he felt anxiety followed by sensory and emotional numbness that still hasn’t gone away. He also developed an autoimmune condition. Online, he found others with some similar experiences. When he looked carefully at the published research, he concluded that it didn’t match the online hype. “I felt very foolish afterwards.”

Some scientists are continuing to investigate these peptides. At Moffitt, Luca is currently studying BPC-157 and he’s keeping an open mind.

Over the next few months, he and his colleagues plan to run a variety of very basic experiments in which they add the peptide to human cells and see how those cells respond. The results could provide additional insight into what, precisely, this molecule is doing in the body—and they may help explain why users have reported everything from striking benefits to lasting harm.

Perhaps one day, BPC-157 will prove to be an effective drug, said Luca. But for now, his advice is to wait on using it and other experimental peptides: “I just worry that people are a little too cavalier in how these are handled.”

Sara Talpos is a Michigan-based journalist covering biomedical research and public health. She’s written for publications including STAT, Science, and Undark, where she’s a contributing editor.

#5020. Zinc: The Missing Link in Anorexia?

After years or decades, problems and patterns can become deeply entrenched. Be a fly on the wall during a sample couples therapy session and witness the slow, deliberate work of changing those patterns for a more enduring love.

Self Tests are all about you. Are you outgoing or introverted? Are you a narcissist? Does perfectionism hold you back? Find out the answers to these questions and more with Psychology Today.

Posted November 4, 2026 | Reviewed by Monica Vilhauer Ph.D.

Over more than three decades of treating children, adolescents, and adults with eating disorders—including anorexia nervosa—I’ve seen the same pattern again and again. A patient arrives with severe anorexia, and they turn out to be severely deficient in one essential mineral: zinc.

So, while anorexia is a complicated disease, one part of treatment is very simple: If you, your child, or your patient has anorexia, the first and best step toward recovery may be correcting a zinc deficiency.

1: Zinc deficiency looks remarkably like anorexia. Imagine I described someone with a poor appetite, weight loss, slowed growth, changes in taste and smell, depression, anxiety, problems concentrating, low energy, social withdrawal, and digestive complaints. Would you think I was describing anorexia nervosa? You’d be right.

But I’d also be describing the symptoms of…zinc deficiency. Yes, many of the hallmark symptoms of anorexia are also classic symptoms of zinc deficiency. That doesn’t prove zinc deficiency causes anorexia. But it raises an obvious question: Could at least some of what we’ve traditionally thought of as the symptoms of anorexia actually be driven—or made worse—by a deficiency of this one nutrient?

2: Zinc deficiency is extremely common in people with anorexia. When researchers measure zinc levels in people with anorexia, they consistently find that a substantial percentage are deficient. Depending on the study and the methods used, at least half—and in some studies many more—show evidence of inadequate zinc status.

3: Remove zinc from an animal’s diet—and it begins to resemble someone with anorexia. In several studies, when scientists fed laboratory animals a diet lacking zinc, the animals ate less and lost weight. Growth slowed. They became less active socially. Females stopped reproducing. In short, they displayed features of anorexia nervosa.

Zinc is involved in hundreds of biological processes throughout the body; it helps regulate the activity of more than 300 enzymes, and it influences thousands of proteins that control growth, repair, metabolism, and communication between cells. In terms of our understanding of anorexia, zinc is crucial for:

Appetite regulation. Zinc participates in the complex network of hormones and signaling molecules that help regulate hunger, satiety, and energy balance. When zinc levels fall, that finely tuned system begins to malfunction. Appetite often declines. Food becomes less appealing. Eating can begin to feel like an obligation rather than a pleasure. This creates a cruel biological cycle.

A person who is already eating too little develops a zinc deficiency. The zinc deficiency further suppresses appetite. Reduced appetite leads to even less food intake. That, in turn, worsens the zinc deficiency.

I’ve seen this cycle happen so many times that I now think of it as one of the central biological engines driving anorexia. Breaking this cycle is one of the reasons correcting zinc deficiency can be so important.

Intra-brain communication. Zinc helps regulate the activity of several neurotransmitter systems that have long been implicated in anorexia, including glutamate and GABA, while also influencing serotonin and other chemical messengers involved in mood and anxiety.

You may be thinking: The biological connection between zinc deficiency and anorexia certainly sounds convincing. But does correcting the deficiency actually help people recover?

For more than four decades, studies on zinc and anorexia point in the same general direction: When people with anorexia receive supplemental zinc, they tend to improve. They gain weight more readily. Their appetite and sense of taste may return. Their mood becomes more stable. Physical development resumes. And, importantly, zinc has generally proved safe and well-tolerated.

One of the most impressive clinical trials enrolled 35 women who had been hospitalized with anorexia. Each patient was randomly assigned to receive either 100 milligrams (mg) of zinc daily or a placebo. The women continued taking zinc or a placebo until they achieved a 10% increase in body mass index (BMI). The results were striking.

The women receiving zinc increased their BMI at twice the rate of those receiving the placebo. Think about what that means. Both groups were hospitalized. Both were receiving expert care for anorexia. Both were working to regain weight. The principal difference between the two groups was that one also received zinc. And that group recovered significantly faster.

Writing in The International Journal of Eating Disorders, the researchers concluded, ‘‘The use of zinc supplementation should be considered in the treatment of AN patients.’’ I agree.

You’ll find citations to many other similarly impressive studies in the references at the end of this blog. The overall message from the research is that correcting zinc deficiency can improve recovery from anorexia nervosa.

Unlike vitamin C or niacin deficiency, zinc deficiency doesn’t cause one distinct, readily diagnosable disease. Instead, it can produce a wide and seemingly unrelated assortment of physical and psychological problems. These can include:

To complicate matters, normal blood zinc does not always mean normal zinc status—because only a tiny fraction of the body’s zinc circulates freely in the blood, and the body works hard to maintain adequate zinc in the bloodstream.

For these reasons, laboratory results must be interpreted in context. A zinc value near the lower end of the reference range may be particularly significant when it appears alongside anorexia, restrictive eating, poor appetite, altered taste, slow healing, frequent infection, anxiety, depression, or other signs associated with deficiency.

Once zinc deficiency has been identified—or is strongly suspected—the next step is correcting it. I generally recommend beginning with therapeutic doses as part of a comprehensive program of targeted nutritional therapy. My protocols are the following:

For adolescents, the typical starting dose is 15 mg of zinc twice daily, for a daily total of 30 mg.

For adults, the typical starting dose is 30 mg twice daily, for a daily total of 60 mg.

After approximately three months, these doses are generally reduced by half—to 15 mg daily for adolescents, and 30 mg daily for adults. This level establishes ongoing maintenance of zinc levels during recovery.

These recommendations should be individualized for the patient. The protocol should also be supervised by a qualified healthcare professional knowledgeable about nutritional medicine.

You can read much more about zinc and anorexia in my new book, Finally Nourished: A Science-Based Plan to Correct Malnutrition in Anorexia Nervosa—and Achieve True and Lasting Recovery.
